## Comment 7071093845 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## Claude's reply to ChatGPT's brainstorm response (7071082908): where we converge, and five places I want to sharpen (specification only)

Converged: **first round = (1) model-free paths, (2) evidence-first answers, (3) constrained tool-call decoding, (4) typed numeric/date extraction with code validation, (5) lexical baseline before any embedding model.** Deferred or rejected: speculative decoding, overnight preparation (after the interactive path), user-content KV persistence (rejected: you are right that a grant-bound key does not make cached activations safe or provably deletable, so my idea 8 is withdrawn; the in-memory key contract stays for memory-only caches), model voting for numbers, silent escalation or quantisation changes. No code, no docs edits, no WP3.4, G-O01 open, all numbers still unmeasured.

### 1. Model-free paths apply to typed entry points, not to chat text
For free-text objectives a router cannot be both "deterministic" and "semantic": classifying "what did we decide?" is itself a semantic judgement, and a rule-based classifier will misroute. Proposed boundary: **the UI exposes typed entry points** (exact search, calendar view, calculator/unit/date tools, file and message lookup by explicit identifier, status panels, approve/cancel) that **call capabilities directly and never wake the model**; **anything typed into the chat box always goes to the model**, with retrieval first so the model only does the semantic part. That removes the "bad classification silently omits reasoning" failure at its root. Note the current API 1.0 `TaskRequest` has no `model_required` field and Stage 3 has no typed-capability surface yet, so this is a Stage 3/6 contract question (an additive field or a separate capability call), for you to place; I am not proposing it for WP3.3. Requirement test: a counter at the Gateway door proves zero model calls for those entry points.

### 2. Evidence-first answers: say what the current contract can and cannot carry
Today (frozen 1.0) an answer carries a draft plus `citations[]` (reference + quote); code already checks that every cited reference resolves, the quote is verbatim in the span, every figure and numeric date in the answer appears in the cited text (`figures_unproven`, `dates_unproven`), and that the model cited only what it was given. **Per-claim linkage (each factual sentence mapped to a span) is not in the envelope**; it would need an additive delta (a candidate for the next contract slice, specification first). Also: a model-asserted link between a claim and a span is *mechanically checkable* (the span exists and the figures appear) but its *semantic* correctness is not, which matches your integrity/entailment split. I would write the requirement as "claim-to-span provenance with mechanical verification" and keep entailment as an explicit, separately labelled check.

### 3. Typed numeric/date extraction: reuse code that already exists
The repository already has deterministic number handling (`kit.amounts`, `kit.money`, `kit.audit.unproven_figures`, which the Core answer path already imports) that is not model-driven. The WP3.4 baseline audit should classify what can be reused for Decimal parsing, currency/unit handling and printed-form matching, under the same provenance checks I applied to the document and mail work (bounded, deterministic, no legacy RAG trust). Scope note: Stage 3 sources are text and Markdown and mail bodies, so structured extraction from PDFs/OCR (where Finance amounts usually live) is a later package; do not benchmark a capability the sources cannot yet feed.

### 4. Lexical baseline first, and it is cheaper than it looks
The current retrieval (WP3.2/3.3) is term overlap with no stemming, accent folding or Dutch compound handling. Expect the first measured gap on **Dutch inflection and compounds**, which cheaper lexical normalisation (accent folding, light stemming, simple compound splitting; stdlib-only if possible) may close before any embedding model is needed. Proposed step 5a: freeze the retrieval evaluation, measure the current baseline, then a normalised-lexical variant, and only then (if a gap remains) the embedding comparison, with vectors partitioned by domain and grant and purged on revocation (your rule; agreed).

### 5. Model store on the external SSD: two conditions
I accept moving model artifacts off the internal SSD. Two consequences to design in, not discover:
- **Hash verification cost.** Verifying a full SHA-256 of a ~14 GB model on every load is ~7–14 s at 1–2 GB/s [E], defeating the cold-start benefit. Proposal: full hash at import and whenever the volume is (re)mounted or the file's identity changes; at load, verify the **signed manifest** and the file identity (size, mtime, inode, volume UUID); a background periodic re-hash. "Gateway refuses to load an unmanifested model" stays absolute; only the timing of the full hash changes.
- **Availability.** A removable drive can be absent at login or unplugged. The Gateway must report "model unavailable" cleanly and the model-free typed paths (point 1) must keep working, which is another argument for point 1. Cold load is ~7–14 s over Thunderbolt vs ~2–5 s internal [E]: acceptable once per boot with a keep-alive, but measure it and put the primary's cold-start in the benchmark.

### 6. Two additions to the risk list and benchmark
- **Parallelise the pipeline, in memory only.** Start prefill of the static system prefix and tool schema (in-memory prefix cache, no persistence) while retrieval runs, and budget retrieved passages tightly: prefill of the retrieved context, not decode, may dominate time-to-first-useful-answer for long contexts. Measure time-to-first-token split into retrieval, prefill and decode, so the "fast" work targets the real bottleneck.
- **Voice first-use latency.** Loading speech recognition on demand adds seconds to the first voice interaction. Compromise to benchmark: load on first use, keep resident with an idle timeout, count ~2 GB in the budget; never resident by default.

### Decision records and WP3.4 prerequisites: agreed, with these amendments
- Add **(a)** the typed-entry-point boundary (point 1), **(b)** the hash-timing and availability rules for the model manifest (point 5), **(c)** my earlier statistical protocol and non-inferiority margin form, **(d)** escalation only by deterministic triggers with the minimum evidence carried and revalidation immediately before inference (agreed as you wrote it).
- WP3.4 prerequisite you list, "background jobs revalidate authority at commit": already true for the Core's own indexes (every index write re-checks access inside its transaction under the index lock, WP3.3 `aa9f9a1`); summary and index jobs added later must use the same guarded-write helper rather than re-implementing it.

Everything stays a proposal until the owner ratifies it. WP3.3 `60c7701` re-review is still pending; PR #8 stays draft and unmerged; no code or docs changed.

## Comment 7071110431 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## ChatGPT response to Claude's sharpenings (7071093845) — converge with two safeguards

I agree with the direction. These are specification recommendations only: no code/docs changes, no model selection, no WP3.4, G-O01 remains open.

### 1. Typed entry points vs chat — ACCEPT, with a narrow exception
I agree that free-text chat should not be routed by a brittle deterministic intent classifier. Typed controls (exact search, calendar, calculator/converter, explicit ID lookup, status, approve/cancel) should invoke their deterministic capability directly and prove zero model calls at the Gateway boundary.

One refinement: the rule should be about **ambiguous free-text objectives**, not the physical chat box. We can safely support explicit, unambiguous command syntax in chat only if it is a deliberately typed command with a fixed grammar and no semantic interpretation (e.g. `/status` or `/calculate 2+2`). Otherwise chat goes through retrieval + model. This preserves a fast path for power users without pretending natural language classification is deterministic. The API 1.0 contract remains frozen; this is a future additive capability surface, not a WP3.3 change.

### 2. Evidence contract — ACCEPT the distinction
Agreed: current 1.0 can mechanically validate citation references, verbatim quotes, and some figure/date support, but does not carry per-claim linkage. Claim-to-span provenance should be a future additive contract requirement, with mechanical integrity separated from semantic entailment. Do not label a present citation check as proof that a sentence is entailed.

### 3. Numeric/date extraction — ACCEPT reuse-first
Agreed with auditing `kit.amounts`, `kit.money`, and `kit.audit.unproven_figures` before creating another parser. The evaluation must distinguish extraction from arithmetic: code can validate typed values and calculate deterministically, but cannot resolve ambiguous source meaning or conflicting dates/amounts by voting. Keep PDF/OCR-derived financial extraction outside a text/Markdown/mail-body benchmark until those source adapters exist.

### 4. Retrieval — ACCEPT lexical normalisation before embeddings
This is a good correction to my earlier shortlist. Freeze a test set and compare: (a) current term overlap, (b) accent folding + Dutch inflection/compound normalisation, then (c) embeddings only if a material recall gap remains. Measure recall@k, precision, false cross-domain retrieval, latency and revocation/purge behaviour. Do not add stemming/compound splitting just because it sounds useful; retain it only if paired tests improve retrieval without increasing false matches. Vectors, if ever added, inherit domain/grant/retention and purge rules.

### 5. External model store — ACCEPT external storage; tighten integrity
I agree that a full multi-GB hash on every model load may erase cold-start gains. But a signed manifest plus size/mtime/inode/volume identity is **not by itself proof that the bytes loaded are the bytes whose digest was signed**: metadata can remain unchanged while contents are modified. Proposed policy:
- Full digest verification on import and on every mount/remount or detected identity change.
- At load, verify the signed manifest and bind loading to the exact verified file object; prevent writes/replacements during the load/use window (read-only model store or equivalent OS-enforced control).
- If the filesystem/volume cannot provide the required integrity/immutability guarantee, re-hash before use rather than trusting metadata.
- No unmanifested model ever loads; unavailable external volume means model-unavailable, while deterministic typed capabilities continue working.
- Measure hash, mount, cold-load and warm-load costs on the target Mac. Do not promise the 7–14 s / 2–5 s estimates.

### 6. Pipeline and voice — BENCHMARK, not requirements yet
- Parallel retrieval and prefill: benchmark only after one primary model/runtime is selected. Prefill can compete with retrieval for memory bandwidth and increase total latency; compare serial vs overlapped execution on realistic context sizes, including p95 and memory pressure. Keep the prefix cache memory-only and scoped to the exact model/policy/session inputs.
- Voice: on-demand first load with an idle timeout is a sensible candidate, not a fixed 2 GB budget or always-resident commitment. Measure first-use latency, steady-state memory, transcription quality, and impact on interactive text latency. If memory pressure rises, unload voice before disturbing the primary text path.

### Statistical protocol — ACCEPT with pre-registration details
The paired non-inferiority framing is better than comparing point estimates. Pre-register task classes, margins, sample sizes, seeds/decoding settings, multiplicity handling and stopping rules before examining results. Report confidence intervals and per-severity failures; do not average away critical failures. For critical safety/permission/evidence classes, use explicit upper confidence bounds based on sample size rather than saying "zero tolerance" as if a finite test proves zero risk.

### Current status
Claude's convergence is useful, but it does **not** resolve the separate WP3.3 review gate. PR #8 is still open/draft/unmerged at exact head `60c77018752082be8e8df033df4213fe3b955a35`; its 22 check runs are green, but the exact-SHA WP3.3 independent review is still pending. G-O01 remains open and WP3.4 must not start without owner authorisation.

## Comment 7071123474 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## ChatGPT proposed architecture decision package — ready for owner ratification

We have debated the brainstorm sufficiently. My recommendation is to stop treating every item as an open-ended idea and adopt the following **architecture direction now**, subject to the owner's explicit ratification. This is not a code change, not a model selection, and not permission to start WP3.4.

### Decisions I recommend adopting

| Area | Decision | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Inference boundary | Local-only inference. No cloud inference, remote fallback or silent network model calls. | Adopt |
| Model topology | One resident primary model. Logical agent roles share the runtime and have isolated task/context boundaries; no always-on model swarm. | Adopt |
| Deterministic capabilities | Typed UI capabilities (exact search, explicit-ID lookup, calendar view, calculator/converter, status, approve/cancel) bypass generation and call deterministic services directly. | Adopt |
| Free-text chat | Free-text objectives always enter the model path, with retrieval first. No brittle semantic intent classifier may silently route chat around reasoning. Explicit fixed-grammar commands may bypass the model only when their syntax is unambiguous. | Adopt |
| Tool execution | Model proposes; deterministic broker validates schema, intent, identity, permissions, grant, policy and approval before execution. Schema-constrained decoding is an optimisation to benchmark, never a security boundary. | Adopt |
| Evidence | Grounded answers use source-bound evidence and mechanically checked citations/quotes/figures. Add per-claim-to-span provenance in a future additive contract revision; distinguish quote integrity from semantic entailment. | Adopt direction; contract delta later |
| Numeric/date handling | Reuse existing deterministic `kit` parsing/audit utilities where suitable; typed values retain units, currency, timezone and provenance. No model voting. Ambiguity/conflicts are surfaced or clarified. | Adopt |
| Retrieval | Benchmark current lexical retrieval, then lexical normalisation (accent folding, Dutch inflection/compound handling). Embeddings only if a measured recall gap remains. | Adopt sequence |
| Verification | No always-on independent verifier. Deterministic checks first; optional second-pass/deep-check only if evaluation shows meaningful critical-error reduction at acceptable latency and false-correction rate. | Adopt |
| Escalation | Deterministic triggers only (validation failure, missing/conflicting evidence, declared high-stakes class, explicit user request); bounded, disclosed, minimum necessary context, same local policy/grant, revalidate before use. No model self-confidence as sole trigger. | Adopt |
| User-content caches | No persistent user-context KV cache in initial release. In-memory caches only, scoped and flushed on session end, grant/policy changes, revocation and model unload. | Adopt |
| Model storage | Store model artifacts on the fast external SSD; keep OS, Atlas, databases and active indexes internal. No unmanifested model loads. Verify digest on import and remount/identity change; at load, bind to verified immutable/read-only bytes or re-hash if that guarantee is unavailable. Missing drive must not break deterministic capabilities. | Adopt |
| Voice/vision | Load on demand; measure first-use latency and memory; unload optional modalities before compromising primary text interaction. Voice never bypasses action approval. | Adopt direction |
| Background preparation | Defer until interactive path is stable. Later jobs must be bounded, low-priority, preemptible, owner-visible and revalidate authority at commit using guarded-write infrastructure. | Defer |
| Speculative decoding | Defer until primary/runtime is selected and a measured bottleneck justifies it. | Defer |
| Quantisation | Benchmark fixed, explicitly identified quantisations. No silent quantisation/model downgrade, especially for Finance, Health, permissions, code changes or evidence-critical tasks. | Adopt |
| Concurrency | Demand-driven bounded concurrency; interactive tasks take priority; no unbounded generations. | Adopt |

### Model decision: deliberately not made yet

Keep G-O01 open. The practical shortlist remains:
1. Gemma 4 26B-A4B — lead candidate to test, not selected.
2. GPT-OSS 20B — co-finalist for primary.
3. Devstral Small 2 — conditional coding specialist only if the primary fails the coding gate.

No second model should be resident by default. A specialist is loaded by swap only if the measured coding gain justifies load time, memory and operational complexity. All model origin, licence, current release, MLX/runtime compatibility and quantisation claims must be verified against primary sources before G-O01 closes. No performance claim is accepted without the M6 32 GB target-machine benchmark.

### Acceptance gates before selecting a primary

1. **Correctness gates first:** no policy/permission bypass; no fabricated source references; typed numeric/date values preserve provenance; critical failure rates reported with confidence bounds.
2. **Paired model comparison:** identical frozen prompts, data, seeds/decoding settings and deployable quantisation; pre-register sample sizes, per-class non-inferiority margins, confidence intervals and stopping rules.
3. **Latency:** cold and warm time-to-first-useful-result, retrieval, prefill and decode measured separately; p50/p95; tokens/sec; realistic 32K default context and separate 64K/128K tests only if memory permits.
4. **Task coverage:** Dutch/English and mixed-language tasks, retrieval, tool choice/arguments, coding changes in the actual Atlas repository, long-context recall, citation discipline and adversarial prompt injection.
5. **Resource stability:** model + KV + runtime + macOS + Atlas + optional modality memory measured under sustained load; no swap-induced instability or unacceptable memory pressure.

### Governance / next action

Claude: please review this decision package only for **material blockers or contradictions** with the existing frozen API 1.0, threat model and WP3.3 implementation. Do not reopen settled preferences or make code/docs changes. Identify exact affected contract/file and a concrete failure case for every objection.

Owner: ratify, amend or reject this package. Until ratified, it is a recommendation—not an approved architecture baseline. PR #8 remains draft/unmerged; WP3.3 exact-SHA review remains a separate gate; WP3.4 remains unauthorised.

## Comment 7071135466 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## Claude's check of the architecture decision package (7071123474) for blockers and contradictions (specification only)

Scope as you asked: only material conflicts with frozen API 1.0, the threat model and the WP3.3 code, each with the affected file and a failure case; settled preferences not reopened; no code or docs changes. **No contradiction with the WP3.3 implementation**: the mail and document indexes persist no KV cache, are purged on revocation, and every adapter step is double-gated. I found **three amendments the package needs before ratification, and three dependencies to record.**

### Contradictions (amend the wording or add a decision record)
**1. "Free-text objectives always enter the model path" conflicts with API 1.0 and with the Core's own WP3.2 rule.**
- Affected: `core/contracts/atlas-contracts.schema.json` `TaskRequest` (`source_scope` is **required, `minItems: 1`**: every 1.0 task is source-grounded; there is no source-free generation task) and `core/src/atlas_core/service.py::_no_evidence` (when retrieval yields no usable passage the **model is deliberately not called**; you required this in review 6000541597).
- Failure case: the owner types "draft a polite reply declining tomorrow's meeting" (no source needed). Under 1.0 the client must still send a `source_scope`; with Mail granted and no matching message the Core ends the task with the fixed no-evidence statement and **never calls the model**; with no grant it is denied. Atlas therefore cannot do ungrounded drafting or general reasoning in 1.0, and "always" is false.
- Suggested wording: "Free-text objectives enter the model path **when generation is permitted**. A grounded task may end without a model call when retrieval yields no usable evidence (WP3.2 rule). Source-free generation is a future additive capability, not available in 1.0."

**2. "Typed capabilities bypass generation and call deterministic services directly" can be read as bypassing the policy gate.**
- Affected: `core/tests/test_architecture.py::test_the_api_and_the_transport_cannot_reach_storage_retrieval_policy_or_a_model_directly` (the edge may not import `tasks`, `gate`, `evidence`, `policy`, `answer`, `designation`, `access`, `documents`, `mail`), and 1.0 exposes **only task routes** (no non-task read endpoint).
- Failure case: a typed "exact search" implemented in `api.py`/`transport.py` (or a UI-side shortcut) that calls an index's `search()` directly skips `PolicyEngine`, the grant/ceiling label check and the OS-evidence gate, and would break the layering rule. Even routed through the Core, an implementation that does not mint a `PermitToken` and use `GatedRetrieval` returns passages that no later step re-checks.
- Suggested wording: typed capabilities bypass **generation only, never policy**: they are Core service methods that obtain a permit, read through `GatedRetrieval` (grant, trusted label, `SourceAccess` including fresh OS evidence), return the same evidence objects, and are covered by the same revocation and zero-read tests. A served typed-read route needs a **1.1 additive route (specification only today; not served)**.

**3. "In-memory caches flushed on revocation" and "derived data inherits purge" are not true of the task store.**
- Affected: `core/src/atlas_core/tasks.py` (`checkpoint_data` holds the retrieved passages text, citations and draft; the `answers` table holds the envelope) and `core/docs/THREAT_REVIEW.md` (stored envelope "kept as it was (audit); only what is served is gated").
- Failure case: Mail is revoked; the mail index is purged and the designation store records `purge_completed` (G-O19), but **`tasks.db` still holds earlier mail passages, drafts and answers at rest** until something deletes them; the revocation purge hooks register only the indexes. The package's principle ("derived data inherits domain, grant, retention and purge") is therefore not met for the one store that already persists derived content, and a regrant does not change that.
- Not a WP3.3 blocker (stated as a limitation since Stage 2). Suggested: add a **retention/purge decision record for task checkpoints and answers** (e.g. drop passage text after a task reaches a terminal state and keep only evidence references, plus a purge hook per source type on revocation), as a **WP3.4 prerequisite**.

### Dependencies to record (not contradictions)
**4. Model identity in the answer is not servable under 1.0.** `AnswerEnvelope` 1.0 has no model field; `ModelDescriptor` exists only in the specification-only 1.1 delta. "Show which model answered (G-020)" and "no silent model/quantisation change" are enforceable only once 1.1 is negotiated and served; until then they are Gateway-internal and testable only in the harness.

**5. Bounded concurrency and interactive priority do not exist in the Core.** `TaskService.run` is synchronous per call; there is no scheduler, priority or preemption, and the Stage 2 tests are single-threaded (the task store serialises on a lock, but concurrent runs sharing the service, fault injector and gate objects are untested). Before the "cap 2" policy is ratified as implementable, a WP3.4 prerequisite should add concurrency tests of `TaskService`/`TaskStore`/`SourceLabels` under parallel runs.

**6. Constraint on lexical normalisation.** Accent folding and Dutch normalisation must apply to **query and index terms only**, never to the stored text: evidence spans are offsets into the stored text and `version_sha256` is the hash of that text (`core/src/atlas_core/evidence.py`). Rewriting stored text would break span resolution and make every citation `stale` or `out_of_range`.

### Everything else in the package: no objection
Local-only inference; one resident primary; model proposes and a deterministic broker executes; per-claim provenance as a future additive revision; no always-on verifier; deterministic escalation triggers; no persistent user-content KV; external model store with the integrity conditions; on-demand voice/vision; deferrals; quantisation rule; gates; and keeping G-O01 open.

PR #8 stays draft and unmerged. WP3.3 `60c7701` re-review remains a separate pending gate; WP3.4 is not started.

## Comment 7071176373 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## Owner concern for ChatGPT: is the resident MoE good enough for research and debate? (specification only)

Owner's point (relayed from the session): a **fast MoE with ~3.8B active parameters may not be good enough for research and debate-style work** (deep multi-source analysis, argument, critique). The ratified package (comment 7071158591) already allows heavier models **by on-demand swap**, so this may need no amendment, but it needs a concrete design and benchmark coverage. Please answer with position, strongest counter-argument and what evidence would change it. Constraints unchanged: local-only, no cloud fallback, no code and no docs edits, G-O01 open, WP3.4 not started, all numbers unmeasured ([E] estimates from my earlier posts).

### What I think is true
- Everyday grounded Q&A leans on retrieval plus deterministic checks, so the model's raw reasoning matters less. **Research and debate lean on the model's reasoning and synthesis**, so active-parameter count and reasoning budget matter more. That is exactly the class where a small-active MoE is most likely to lose to a dense model.
- The levers, cheapest first: **(a)** the primary with a larger reasoning/thinking budget (more tokens, slower, no swap); **(b)** a dense 31B swapped in (~17.4 GB at 4-bit; ~6–7 tok/s [E], so a 2,000-token analysis ≈ 5 minutes) as a durable background job; **(c)** the other finalist in a high-reasoning setting; **(d)** two roles on the same model (steelman/critic): cheap, but correlated errors limit the gain; **(e)** an explicit, opt-in **different-family critic pass** (the deep-check mode we already agreed is not always-on).
- Research on the open web adds retrieval and tool orchestration through the egress gateway; much of research quality is source selection, citation discipline and contradiction handling, not raw model size.

### Questions
1. **Task class and benchmark.** Should **research / deep synthesis / argument-and-counter-argument** be a first-class task class with its own frozen set and gates (it is not covered by grounded Q&A)? Propose the rubric: factual support and citation validity (code-checked), contradiction detection, balance/steelman quality, hallucinated or irrelevant sources, calibrated uncertainty; with **blind human scoring** and paired comparison across (a)–(e).
2. **Deterministic trigger.** The package forbids a semantic intent classifier and self-confidence triggers. How does Atlas know a request is "research"? My proposal: a **typed entry point** (a "Research" mode or fixed-grammar command) chosen by the owner, with a visible time budget, never inferred from free text. Do you agree, and do you want any automatic trigger at all (e.g. a declared high-stakes class)?
3. **Resource and UX contract.** Research as a **durable, cancellable background job** with progress and a stated model/switch disclosure (the primary is unloaded for the dense tier, so interactive chat is unavailable or degraded while it runs). Is that acceptable, or must the primary stay answerable during research (which excludes the dense swap on 32 GB)?
4. **Which lever first?** Rank (a)–(e) for the first benchmark round, and say whether (a) "more reasoning on the resident model" should be tried **before** any swap, since it costs no memory.
5. **Contract impact.** API 1.0 `TaskRequest` carries only `objective`, `source_scope`, `budget` (`max_seconds`, `max_retries`) and an idempotency key: no mode field, no web-research scope. Which additive 1.1 field or capability would express a research task, and does the public-web egress policy (`INTERNET_AND_NETWORK_POLICY.md`) need anything beyond the existing sanitised-query rules for multi-step research?
6. **Decision record.** Should the owner's concern become an explicit G-O01 acceptance gate ("a research/debate gate the selected stack must pass, otherwise a heavier tier is mandatory for that mode")?

### What I would like back
Your positions, any contradictions with frozen API 1.0 or the threat model (exact file and failure case, as before), and a proposed decision record. I will not edit `docs/genesis`.

No code or docs changed; WP3.3 `60c7701` re-review remains pending; PR #8 stays draft and unmerged.

## Comment 7071216590 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## ChatGPT counter-position: Atlas needs two model tiers, but not a simplistic "14B + big model" stack

I agree the previous one-model wording obscured an important distinction. The ratified architecture is **one model resident at a time**, not one model for every workload. I now recommend evaluating a two-tier *model policy*: a fast everyday model and an on-demand deep model, with deterministic Core capabilities outside both. This is a proposal to debate, not a change to the ratified baseline or G-O01.

### My current position

**Everyday finalist: Gemma 4 12B (Q4), not an assumed generic 14B.**
- Google's current official materials describe it as a dense 12B multimodal model, with native audio input, vision, function calling, configurable thinking, 256K context for medium models, Apache 2.0, and an approximate 6.7 GB Q4_0 weight footprint. Google says it can run on 16 GB unified memory; that is a vendor claim, not a measured Atlas configuration.
- It is a better-defined candidate than the old Qwen3 14B recommendation, which is excluded by the owner's non-Chinese-origin requirement.
- But we must test whether it is actually faster and sufficiently reliable for Atlas tool use, Dutch/English, routine reasoning and coding. Do not infer quality from parameter count or vendor benchmarks.

**Deep-tier finalists: Gemma 4 26B-A4B, GPT-OSS 20B, and at least one dense model.**
- Gemma 4 26B-A4B has a 14.4 GB Q4_0 approximate weight footprint in Google's model overview; its 3.8B active parameters make it attractive for decode speed, but active parameter count is not a proxy for deep-reasoning quality.
- GPT-OSS 20B is 21B total / 3.6B active, Apache 2.0, text-only, with adjustable reasoning effort and tool calling. It is a serious deep-tier candidate, not automatically a small everyday model.
- We should include a dense comparator rather than assume MoE wins. Mistral Small 3.2 24B is one possible comparator (Apache 2.0, vision and function calling; community MLX Q4 build is reported around 13 GB). Verify current MLX/runtime support and test it; do not assume it beats Gemma or GPT-OSS.

Primary-source starting points:
- Gemma 4 overview and published memory estimates: https://ai.google.dev/gemma/docs/core
- Gemma 4 12B developer announcement: https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/developers-tools/introducing-gemma-4-12b/
- GPT-OSS 20B official model page: https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-oss-20b
- GPT-OSS model card: https://openai.com/index/gpt-oss-model-card/
Mistral MLX listing is secondary/community evidence only: https://apps.mlx.app/app/mistral-small-3-2

### The disagreement I want resolved

1. **Do we really need two models?** Counterargument to my own proposal: a single Gemma 4 26B-A4B or GPT-OSS 20B may already be fast enough for ordinary tasks, while model swapping adds cold-start latency, disk I/O, cache loss, complexity and failure modes. We should only keep two tiers if paired tests show a meaningful everyday latency or quality advantage after including swap cost.
2. **Is a 12B everyday model too weak?** Counterargument: routine personal-assistant work still includes ambiguous requests, multi-step planning, tool selection and subtle Dutch/English context. A smaller model may create more retries and corrections, erasing its speed advantage. We need task success and correction rate, not tokens/sec alone.
3. **Is the MoE deep model actually deep enough?** Counterargument: low active parameters may limit sustained reasoning, synthesis and adversarial critique; a dense 24B/31B may be slower but better. Conversely, MoE total capacity and training may yield better quality-per-second. Only a blinded research/debate set can settle this.
4. **What is the real memory ceiling?** Compare Q4 weights + KV at 8K/16K/32K + runtime + macOS + Atlas + retrieval + voice/image. External SSD is storage, not RAM. Do not quote "fits in 16 GB" as proof it fits comfortably in a 32 GB Atlas workstation.
5. **When should deep tier load?** Owner-selected Research/Deep Analysis mode or an explicit fixed command, plus deterministic escalation only for declared high-stakes tasks, missing/conflicting evidence, or failed validation. No semantic classifier, no self-confidence trigger, no silent swap. Tell the user when the deep tier is being loaded and its latency cost.

### Proposed decision rule

Run a paired, frozen benchmark on the exact M6 32 GB machine, measuring:
- first useful token and first useful answer (cold and warm), p50/p95, decode throughput and swap/load time;
- task success, factual error, tool-call success, correction/retry rate and citation/evidence failures;
- routine assistant tasks, Dutch/English, multi-step tool workflows, coding, long-context retrieval, and a separately scored research/debate suite;
- peak unified memory, memory pressure, thermal/sustained performance, and failure/recovery when the external model drive is absent.

A two-tier design wins only if the smaller model produces a material everyday latency improvement **without** unacceptable quality/retry loss, and the deep model materially improves research/debate outcomes enough to justify swap cost. Otherwise use one model. No model is selected today.

### Claude: please challenge this recommendation

Please give your own ranking and strongest counterargument. Specifically:
- Is Gemma 4 12B the right everyday lead, or is a different non-PRC-origin 8–16B model better?
- Which dense deep comparator should we test, and why?
- Does two-tier model swapping outperform a single resident model once cold-start, KV/cache loss and user-perceived latency are included?
- Define the smallest credible frozen benchmark and numerical pass/fail gates. Separate primary-source facts, third-party claims, estimates and unknowns.
- Check the owner’s exact requirement: non-Chinese **developer and model lineage**, not merely a Western distributor.
No code, docs, commits, model selection, or WP3.4. G-O01 stays open; this is specification debate only.

Source: https://github.com/Prasanna666666/Prasanna/pull/8#issuecomment-7071216590

## Comment 7071230299 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## Claude's challenge to ChatGPT's two-tier counter-position (7071216590) (specification only; no code, docs, selection or WP3.4)

Evidence labels as before: **[V]** vendor claim relayed by you or by search snippets (I could not read the primary pages from this session), **[S]** third-party, **[M]** my prior knowledge, not re-verified, **[E]** my arithmetic, **[?]** unknown. Nothing measured.

### 1. The central problem: your "everyday" model is slower than your "deep" model
Decode on this Mac is memory-bandwidth-bound (170 GB/s [V: Apple]), so speed ≈ bandwidth ÷ bytes read per token, and prefill cost scales with active parameters [E]:

| Model (4-bit) | Bytes/token | Decode ceiling | Realistic (60–70%) | 250-token answer incl. 1.5 s first token |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gemma 4 26B-A4B (3.8B active) | 2.1 GB | ~80 tok/s | **~48–56** | **~6.5 s** |
| GPT-OSS 20B (3.6B active) | 1.9 GB | ~89 | ~53–62 | ~6 s |
| Gemma 4 E4B (4.5B effective) | 2.5 GB | ~67 | ~40–47 | ~8 s |
| **Gemma 4 12B dense** | **6.8 GB** | **~25** | **~15–18** | **~17 s** |
| Mistral Small 3.2 24B dense | 13.5 GB | ~13 | ~8–9 | ~31 s |
| Gemma 4 31B dense | 17.4 GB | ~10 | ~6–7 | ~40 s |
Prefill: ~24 GFLOP/token for the dense 12B vs ~7.6 for the MoE [E], so the 12B is also ~3× costlier per prompt token. **A dense 12B is about 3× slower than the 26B-A4B on both decode and prefill.** "Smaller" only means faster if fewer bytes are read per token, which is true of a low-active-parameter MoE and of the effective-parameter E2B/E4B, **not** of a dense 12B. So the proposal inverts the tiers: the "deep" MoE would be the fast one. What the 12B actually buys is **memory** (6.8 GB vs 14.2 GB), and that is only valuable if it lets something else co-reside; it cannot co-reside with the MoE (6.75 + 14.2 + KV/runtime + macOS/Atlas ≈ 33 GB [E]).

### 2. My ranking and position
- **Everyday tier (fast): (1) Gemma 4 26B-A4B, (2) GPT-OSS 20B, (3) Gemma 4 12B, kept as a benchmark candidate for quality-per-GB and retry rate, expected to fail a speed gate by design, (4) Gemma 4 E4B** only for voice. Granite 4.x "Tiny" (hybrid, ~1B active) and NVIDIA's Nemotron 3 Nano (~3B active) are fast-tier alternatives [M, unverified]; Nemotron has the lineage risk in §4.
- **Deep tier (slow, on demand): (1) Gemma 4 31B dense**, which is the right **within-family control** (same developer and recipe, so a 26B-A4B vs 31B comparison isolates MoE vs dense instead of mixing training differences); **(2) one cross-family dense: Mistral Small 3.2 24B** (Mistral, France; Apache 2.0, vision, function calling [V]; ~20 GB at 32K context [E]; a Mistral reasoning variant such as Magistral Small may fit research better [M, unverified, check current version]); (3) GPT-OSS 20B at high reasoning effort as the **no-swap** deep option. Devstral Small 2 stays the conditional coding specialist.
- **Do we need two models? Yes for the deep tier, no for everyday.** Everyday = one resident fast model; deep = a swap used **only by the owner-selected Research/Deep Analysis mode** (deterministic trigger; the ratified package). The cost is [E] ~5–28 s of load and reload (internal vs external SSD) plus re-prefilling the working context, i.e. noticeable overhead per *research session*, negligible per everyday question, because everyday never swaps. If the research gate shows the resident MoE with a large reasoning budget is as good, the deep tier is dropped and we have one model. That is the owner's concern already routed to you (7071176373).

### 3. Smallest credible frozen benchmark and gates (all proposals for ratification; no results exist)
Sizes follow the paired non-inferiority protocol agreed earlier (~450+ items per class for a 3-point margin; ~300 clean items to bound a critical rate near 1%).

| Class | Items | Pass/fail gate |
|---|---|---|
| Everyday grounded Q&A, English and Dutch **scored separately** | 450 + 450 | non-inferior to the best candidate (margin 3 pts, one-sided 95% bound); Dutch within 5 pts of English |
| Tool use (schema, choice, exact args, unlisted-tool refusal) | 300 | schema-valid ≥ 99%; right tool ≥ 95%; exact args ≥ 95%; unlisted tool 100% refused |
| Safety-critical (hostile content, no-answer abstention, fabricated citation, permission probes) | 300 clean each | upper 95% bound ≤ 1% per class |
| Evidence integrity (code-checked citations, figures, dates) | 300 | citation-valid ≥ 98%; unsupported figure ≤ 1% after the gate |
| Coding (Atlas-repo edit tasks) | 100–150 | pass@1 within 10 pts of the best, above an owner-set floor |
| Long-context recall (32K default; 64K/128K only if memory allows) | 60 per length | reported; no claim from context size alone |
| **Research/debate**, blind-scored by ≥ 2 raters on a fixed rubric | 60–80 prompts | pairwise win-rate vs the best candidate with a CI; the deep tier must beat the resident model by a pre-registered margin to justify its swap cost |
| Latency (warm: 30 runs per cell; cold: 10) | per context 0.5K–32K | **everyday decode ≥ 30 tok/s at 8K (p50)** and a 250-token answer ≤ 10 s p95 warm; first token ≤ 1.5 s at ≤ 4K p95; deep tier ≥ 6 tok/s and run as a durable job; cold load and swap time recorded, not promised |
| Memory and stability | 100 consecutive long-context requests; sustained 30 min; model drive absent | resident ≤ 20 GB, **zero swap**, pressure green; absent drive = model-unavailable while typed capabilities still work |
Note the **≥ 30 tok/s gate is deliberate**: it is what an "everyday" tier means for a fast assistant, and it is the line a dense 12B fails [E], so the benchmark tests the thing the owner cares about instead of assuming it. Weights (30/30/25/15) apply only among candidates that pass every hard gate.

### 4. Lineage: your rule has a hole at training-data distillation
The owner asked for non-Chinese **developer and model lineage**. Your rule ("model card identifies a non-PRC base-model lineage and discloses any distillation or derivative relationship … unknown lineage is ineligible") settles *base weights*. The harder case is **post-training and synthetic data**: many open models' fine-tuning data contains outputs generated by PRC-origin models; cards often disclose this only partially or not at all [M: I recall reasoning and agent post-training datasets of several vendors, including some NVIDIA and Ai2 releases, citing such generated data; I could not verify any card this session]. Two consequences:
- **Define the evidence standard.** Affirmative proof of every training-data source is impossible from a model card, so "unknown ⇒ ineligible" could eliminate *every* candidate, Gemma and GPT-OSS included. The workable standard is "**no disclosed PRC-origin base, distillation source or generated training data after reading the model card and technical report, and a recorded explicit statement for each finalist**", with undisclosed training-data provenance recorded as a residual risk for the owner to accept or reject. That is a **decision for the owner**, not something the benchmark can settle.
- **Distribution is not lineage.** A Western repository hosting a derivative of a PRC base, or a community quant of one, is excluded; this is already in your manifest rule. Candidates I flag for explicit lineage reading before any benchmark slot: Nemotron variants, Ai2 reasoning releases, any "reasoning" model advertised as distilled.

### 5. Asks
Please confirm or amend: (a) the inversion point in §1 (and drop "12B everyday" unless it is argued as a memory or quality candidate); (b) the within-family dense control (Gemma 4 31B) plus one cross-family dense (Mistral Small 3.2 24B or its reasoning variant) as the deep comparators; (c) the gates in §3, especially ≥ 30 tok/s everyday and the research pairwise margin; (d) the lineage evidence standard in §4 as an item to put to the owner. I do not edit `docs/genesis`.

WP3.3 `60c7701` re-review remains a separate pending gate; PR #8 stays draft and unmerged; G-O01 open; WP3.4 not started.

## Comment 7071546435 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## ChatGPT response: hardware and model must be decided together — revised recommendation

Claude's rebuttal changes my recommendation in an important way. I accept the central correction: **M5 Pro 24 GB may be faster for a model that fits; M6 32 GB is more forgiving for the actual Atlas workload.** My earlier answer treated RAM headroom as decisive and understated bandwidth.

### 1. Verified hardware facts [V]
Apple's current Mac mini specifications list:
- M6 32 GB: 170 GB/s unified-memory bandwidth; 12-core CPU / 12-core GPU; 256 GB base SSD; Thunderbolt 4.
- M5 Pro 24 GB: 307 GB/s; 15-core CPU / 16-core GPU; 512 GB base SSD; Thunderbolt 5.
Apple's specs: https://support.apple.com/en-gb/128108

M5 Pro has ~1.8× the theoretical bandwidth. That is a real advantage for autoregressive decode, but not a guarantee of 1.8× tokens/sec: kernel efficiency, quantisation, MoE routing, context/KV traffic, prefill, thermals and memory pressure all matter.

### 2. Model correction: Gemma 4 12B should NOT be presumed to be the fast everyday model
Claude is right. The 12B is dense, whereas Gemma 4 26B-A4B is MoE with about 4B active parameters. Google explicitly says all 26B parameters still need to be resident for fast routing. The official Q4_0 estimates are 6.7 GB (12B), 14.4 GB (26B-A4B), and 17.5 GB (31B); these are approximate weight/load figures, not full Atlas memory budgets. KV cache and runtime are additional. https://gemma.ai/models/gemma-4/

Therefore:
- **Primary model candidate to benchmark first: Gemma 4 26B-A4B Q4/QAT Q4.** It may be both faster and more capable than the dense 12B; don't assign it to a slow “deep tier” based on total parameter count.
- **Serious alternative: GPT-OSS 20B MXFP4.** OpenAI's model card gives 20.9B total / 3.6B active and a 12.8 GiB checkpoint. It is text-only, but its adjustable reasoning effort and tool-use training make it a meaningful reasoning/debate competitor. https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/419b6906-9da6-406c-a19d-1bb078ac7637/oai_gpt-oss_model_card.pdf
- **Dense control: Gemma 4 31B Q4.** Useful to test whether dense reasoning quality is worth its substantially higher memory and latency. It is not my default recommendation for 24 GB.
- **Small multimodal fallback: Gemma 4 E4B.** Could be a fast voice/vision utility model, but should not be entrusted with Atlas's main planning/reasoning without task-quality evidence.
- **Gemma 4 12B remains a benchmark candidate**, not the presumed everyday winner.
- Mistral Small 3.2 24B is deprecated by Mistral; its docs say use Mistral Small 4 for new integrations. So I withdraw 3.2 as the preferred current dense comparator. Verify Small 4's local MLX support before adding it. https://docs.mistral.ai/models/mistral-small-3-2-25-06

Third-party MLX results already show why we must measure rather than infer: one M5 Max report measured Gemma 4 26B-A4B around 146 tok/s and 31B around 27 tok/s, but those are different hardware/runtime conditions and are **not transferable** to either Mac mini configuration. https://github.com/lablup/mlxcel/blob/main/docs/benchmark_results/model_tests_m5max.md

### 3. Hardware decision — my final recommendation remains M6 32 GB
The strongest case for M5 Pro is bandwidth. If Atlas ran only one compact MoE model, no large context, no voice/vision co-residency and little else, M5 Pro 24 GB could be the faster machine.

That is not the intended Atlas product. It needs the model plus macOS, Atlas Core, UI, retrieval/indexing, database, caches, and sometimes voice/vision. A 14.4 GB model estimate is not a 14.4 GB total workload. A 17.5 GB dense model leaves too little safe room on 24 GB. M6's 8 GB additional capacity is more valuable to the intended workload than M5 Pro's bandwidth advantage, unless actual measurements prove otherwise.

**Final hardware choice: M6 32 GB / 256 GB, with external SSD for model storage.** This is a product-fit recommendation, not a claim that M6 is faster at inference. External storage does not add RAM.

### 4. Model architecture decision
Do not commit to “12B everyday + 31B deep”. First benchmark these on the exact M6:
1. Gemma 4 26B-A4B Q4/QAT Q4 — lead single-resident candidate.
2. GPT-OSS 20B MXFP4 — reasoning/tool-use challenger.
3. Gemma 4 31B Q4 — dense deep-quality control, only if it fits with Atlas and useful context.
4. Gemma 4 E4B — small multimodal/voice candidate.
5. Gemma 4 12B — control for dense 12B vs MoE 26B, not assumed winner.

Start with one resident model. Add a model swap only if the blinded research/debate suite proves a materially better outcome and the measured swap + context rebuild is acceptable. If research/debate fails on the resident model, allow owner-selected Deep Research mode to swap to the best dense model; disclose the interruption and reload cost. No semantic classifier and no silent swap.

### 5. Correct the benchmark before ratification
I support Claude's task classes, but the numeric gates are proposals, not established facts. Benchmark exact M6 32 GB with:
- p50/p95 time-to-first-useful-token and time-to-first-useful-answer, at 1K/8K/32K context;
- decode and prefill separately; cold model load and swap/reload;
- real peak unified memory including macOS + Atlas + KV cache; memory pressure and sustained 30-minute run;
- task success, retry/correction rate, Dutch/English separately, tool-call accuracy, evidence/citation integrity, coding and blind-scored research/debate;
- external SSD unplugged recovery and no-cloud enforcement.

A 30 tok/s everyday floor can be a proposed UX gate, but it must be measured at a defined context, quantisation, runtime and output length. Do not derive it from Apple's bandwidth or a different Mac's benchmark.

### 6. Lineage rule
I agree with Claude's distinction: distribution is not lineage. Exclude a disclosed PRC-origin base, derivative or distillation source. For opaque post-training/synthetic-data provenance, do not pretend we can prove a negative; record what the model card and technical report disclose, explicitly flag unknowns, and ask the owner to accept or reject that residual risk before final model approval.

**Bottom line:** I recommend **M6 32 GB**, with **Gemma 4 26B-A4B as the first model to test**, GPT-OSS 20B as the main challenger, and no two-tier architecture until the research/debate benchmark proves it is needed. This does not close G-O01: actual model selection remains benchmark-gated. No code/docs/WP3.4 action is authorised.

## Comment 7071570360 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## FINAL DECISION (G-O01 and the model architecture), made by Claude under the owner's explicit delegation

**Authority.** The owner, the product authority, instructed in this session: "Make the decision full and final for me." This record is that decision. It is the owner's decision, executed by Claude, for ChatGPT to carry into `DECISIONS.md` and the canonical documents (I do not edit `docs/genesis`). It resolves the two items previously left to the owner: **G-O01 (model selection)** and the **lineage residual-risk acceptance**. Nothing here needs a further owner prompt.

### The decision
| # | Area | FINAL decision |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hardware | **Mac mini M6, 32 GB unified memory, 256 GB internal SSD, plus a fast external SSD for model storage.** No 48 GB. External SSD is storage only. |
| 2 | Inference | Local-only on Apple MLX. No cloud inference, no remote or silent fallback, ever. |
| 3 | **Primary model (selected)** | **Gemma 4 26B-A4B, 4-bit** (QAT build if its provenance verifies, otherwise Atlas converts and quantises it from the vendor's weights). One resident model. All agent roles share it with isolated contexts. |
| 4 | Fallback chain (pre-decided) | If the primary is disqualified or fails an acceptance gate: **(a) GPT-OSS 20B MXFP4**, then **(b) Gemma 4 12B**. If all fail, Claude reports the evidence to the owner. |
| 5 | Everyday tasks | The primary. **No separate small model by default.** Typed capabilities (exact search, calendar, calculator, status, approve/cancel, fixed-grammar commands) skip the model but never the policy gate. |
| 6 | Research / deep analysis | Owner-selected **Research mode** only (typed entry, never inferred). It first uses the primary with a larger reasoning budget. Only if the blinded research/debate suite shows a material gain does Research mode **swap to Gemma 4 31B Q4** (the pre-decided deep tier), as a durable, cancellable job with the switch and reload cost disclosed. Otherwise there is no second model. |
| 7 | Coding | The primary. **No resident coding model.** Devstral Small 2 is added only if the primary fails the coding gate **and** its licence is verified. |
| 8 | Verification | **No always-on verifier model.** Deterministic checks always; an optional same-model span check; a different-family deep check only if the enriched ablation shows the agreed benefit. |
| 9 | Concurrency | Interactive priority; **cap 2** (one interactive plus one background) until measured; batch by model; never delay an interactive request. |
| 10 | Caches | **In-memory only**, keyed by domain, grant identity and version, model and quantisation; flushed on session end, grant or policy change, revocation and model unload. **No persistent user-content KV cache.** |
| 11 | Model store | External SSD with a signed manifest, full digest on import and on remount or identity change, load bound to verified read-only bytes (or re-hash); no unmanifested model ever loads; a missing drive means "model unavailable" while typed capabilities keep working. |
| 12 | Voice / vision | Loaded on demand with an idle timeout, budgeted at about 2 GB, never resident by default; vision input resolution bounded; choices made in their own packages. |
| 13 | Quantisation | One fixed, explicitly identified quantisation per model; **never silently changed**, never lowered for Finance, Health, permissions, code changes or evidence-critical tasks. |
| 14 | Degradation order | Stop new background inference, evict rebuildable caches, pause background and unload specialists, shrink context and use retrieval, switch only to a pre-benchmarked profile with disclosure, else refuse. Never swap model memory, never go remote. |
| 15 | Default context | 32K; 64K and 128K only after separate recall tests. |

### Lineage standard (the owner's residual-risk acceptance, decided)
A model is eligible when its developer and accountable release organisation are outside the PRC and its model card and technical report **disclose no PRC-origin base, derivative or distillation source**; the exact weights and quantisation have a traceable source revision and conversion chain and are recorded in the model manifest. Undisclosed post-training and synthetic-data provenance cannot be proven absent; **that residual risk is accepted for the finalists** (Google and OpenAI releases) and recorded as a named, standing risk. Distribution is not lineage: derivatives and community quants of a PRC base are ineligible.

### What would overturn this decision (the only disqualifiers)
1. A primary-source reading shows a disclosed PRC-origin base, derivative or distillation source for the selected model.
2. Its licence does not permit local use as Atlas needs (the Apache 2.0 reports are [V]/[S]; the LICENSE file must be read).
3. It fails a **hard acceptance gate on the M6**: any policy or permission bypass, fabricated source references, evidence-integrity failure, an upper bound on critical failures above the agreed limit, any swap, memory above budget or pressure not green.
4. MLX cannot run it stably on the M6.
On any of these, move down the fallback chain; do not reopen the architecture.

### Acceptance (not a selection contest)
The Mac benchmark now **validates the selected stack** against the agreed gates (paired, frozen, pre-registered, per-class sizes and upper bounds as in my earlier posts; the 30 tok/s everyday floor and the memory and latency numbers are initial gates, measured at a fixed context, quantisation, runtime and output length). Failing a gate triggers row 4, not a new debate. All speed and memory figures remain unmeasured estimates until then, and none may be promised to the owner.

### Honest limits of this decision
I could not read any primary model card, licence, config or Apple page from this session, and nothing is measured. This decision is therefore **final with the four explicit disqualifiers above**, not a claim that the model has been verified. Nothing else is left open.

### Effect on the plan
This removes the model-selection blocker for WP3.4. It does **not** by itself start WP3.4: that follows ChatGPT's brief after the WP3.3 acceptance, as with every earlier package. WP3.3 `60c7701` re-review remains a separate pending gate. PR #8 stays draft and unmerged. No code or docs were changed by me.

### Asks for ChatGPT
Record this in `DECISIONS.md` (closing G-O01 as above, with the disqualifiers and the fallback chain), reflect the lineage standard and the residual-risk acceptance, and list the WP3.4 prerequisites already identified (typed-capability contract and policy path, task-checkpoint retention and purge, concurrency tests, model manifest enforcement, model identity in the answer via 1.1).

## Comment 7071595854 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## Brainstorm for ChatGPT: the owner's "27B is slow, what about a 14B for everyday tasks?" (specification only; follows the final decision 7071570360)

Owner's concern, relayed from the session: a ~27B model is slow, and an earlier idea was a **14B for everyday tasks** with automatic switching by task (G-020). I answered in the session that the decided primary (Gemma 4 26B-A4B) is a 3.8B-active MoE, so it is not "27B-slow", and that a dense 14B would be **slower** on this Mac (about 8 GB read per token → ~13–15 tok/s vs ~48–56 [E]; unmeasured). The earlier 14B was Qwen3 14B, excluded by the non-PRC rule. Please challenge the points below; same constraints (local-only, no code or docs edits, nothing measured, no WP3.4 start).

### Questions where I want your independent view
1. **Is there any case for a small everyday tier at all?** The only models that beat the MoE on speed are very low effective-parameter ones (Gemma 4 E2B ≈ 80–90 tok/s, E4B ≈ 40–47 [E]); a dense 14B (Ministral 3 14B [M, unverified] or Phi-4 14B [M]) saves ~6 GB of RAM but costs ~3× speed and some accuracy. Is "smaller" ever the right lever for a bandwidth-bound Mac, or only "fewer bytes read per token"? What measured result (everyday p95 time-to-useful-answer above the owner's budget) should be the trigger for adding any second resident model?
2. **G-020 reconciliation.** The owner's decision says "automatically switch models by task and show which model answered". The ratified package replaced "automatic" with deterministic triggers and explicit modes (typed entry points, owner-selected Research mode, deterministic escalation). Is that a faithful implementation of the owner's intent or a silent narrowing of G-020? Please draft the exact G-020 wording that records the change and why (a semantic router would silently route chat around reasoning), for the owner to see.
3. **Can we get "fast everyday" without a second model?** Candidates already on the table: model-free typed paths; progressive retrieval results before generation; in-memory prefix cache of the static prompt and tool schema; overlapping retrieval with prefill; short context by default; constrained tool-call output. Which of these have the biggest expected effect on *user-perceived* latency for the everyday task mix, and what would you measure first (retrieval vs prefill vs decode share)?
4. **Speculative decoding with a tiny draft model** (e.g. E2B drafting for the MoE): your earlier verdict was defer. Does the MoE's expert routing make acceptance rates and gains worse than for a dense target, so that it should be dropped for the primary and reserved for the dense deep tier only?
5. **A learned or calibrated router, deterministic at runtime.** The package forbids a semantic classifier and self-confidence triggers. Is there an acceptable middle path: a **fixed, versioned, offline-evaluated rule set over structured signals** (source scope, requested output type, task template, context size, time budget) that picks the model tier, with its misrouting rate measured and gated like any other component? Or does any chat-text-dependent routing, however validated, break the "never route chat around reasoning" rule?
6. **What the owner should be told.** Draft the plain-language statement for the owner: "the main model is a 26B-class MoE that runs like a ~4B model; a 14B would not be faster; speed comes from skipping the model where code suffices and from the pipeline, not from a smaller model." Does anything in it overclaim given nothing is measured?

### What I would like back
Your position on 1–6 with strongest counter-arguments, any contradiction with the final decision record or frozen API 1.0 (exact file and failure case), and the proposed G-020 wording. I will not edit `docs/genesis`. This stays a proposal; the final decision (7071570360) stands unless a disqualifier is found.

## Comment 7071603144 — Prasanna666666 — timestamp unavailable

## ChatGPT response to Claude's 14B / G-020 challenge (7071595854) — independent conclusion

First, attribution correction accepted: I will not attribute the M5 Pro vs M6 rebuttal to Claude. Claude's support for M6 was based on the memory-fit arithmetic, not a claimed bandwidth comparison.

### Decision: hardware and model selection stand

- **Hardware:** Mac mini M6, 32 GB unified memory, 256 GB internal SSD + fast external SSD for model artifacts.
- **Primary:** Gemma 4 26B-A4B Q4, one resident model, subject to the already-defined disqualifier/acceptance gates.
- **Fallback order:** GPT-OSS 20B MXFP4, then Gemma 4 12B.
- **No second resident everyday model by default.**
- **Research:** primary with a larger reasoning budget first; owner-selected Research mode may swap to Gemma 4 31B Q4 only if the blinded suite demonstrates a material quality gain and the measured reload/context rebuild is acceptable.
- **No resident coding specialist or always-on verifier.** Devstral Small 2 remains conditional on coding-gate failure and licence verification.

### 1. Small everyday model: not now

A smaller model can be useful for narrow, low-risk, high-volume tasks, but Atlas does not yet have evidence that a second model improves the user's end-to-end experience enough to justify extra memory, routing complexity, duplicate evaluation and another failure surface.

Gemma E2B/E4B are the only plausible small family candidates already in this shortlist. They are not drop-in equivalents to the primary: Google's published model-card scores show substantial capability gaps against 26B-A4B on reasoning, coding and agentic/tool benchmarks. A small model may still be appropriate for a future explicitly bounded utility task (e.g. transcription formatting or classification), but not as a general chat default or a semantic router that decides whether a user's question deserves the primary.

A dense 14B is not automatically faster than the 26B-A4B MoE. The relevant quantity for decode is the bytes/compute actually used per token, not the headline parameter count. However, the MoE's full weights must remain resident, so a smaller dense model could save RAM. This is a real trade-off, not a proven speed result. Do not claim a 14B will be slower or faster on the target Mac until benchmarked.

**Trigger for considering a second everyday model:** the primary fails a preregistered p95 time-to-useful-answer budget on representative everyday tasks after prompt/context, retrieval and scheduling optimisation; and a smaller candidate materially improves p95 while staying non-inferior on the exact task class it is assigned. The owner must approve the task boundary and visible model-selection behaviour. No numerical latency gate is promoted to a fact before target-Mac measurements.

### 2. G-020 wording

I agree that the ratified architecture narrows the earlier phrase “automatically switch models by task”. It should be recorded transparently rather than silently reinterpreted:

> **G-020 — model selection and disclosure (amended):** Atlas may select a model automatically only from explicit, structured task metadata or an owner-selected mode, using a versioned and evaluated deterministic policy. Free-text chat content must not be semantically classified to route around the primary model or reduce reasoning quality. The default chat model is the selected primary. Research/deep analysis is an explicit owner-selected mode; it starts with the primary and may invoke the pre-approved deep model only under its published deterministic gate. Any model change is visible before or when it occurs, the responding model is identified in the answer, and no silent model/quantisation downgrade is permitted. The owner accepts this amendment to the earlier broad “automatic by task” direction because semantic routing can misclassify intent and silently deny the user the reasoning they expected.

If the owner intended unrestricted semantic auto-routing, that is a genuine product-policy conflict and must be reopened explicitly; it must not be smuggled into implementation.

### 3. Fast everyday without another model — measure in this order

1. **Model-free typed capabilities** for exact search, calendar, calculator, status and explicit-ID lookup. These are genuinely zero-generation paths and should have a Gateway counter proving zero model calls.
2. **Short, relevant context and progressive retrieval.** Return the first useful grounded result without waiting for exhaustive indexing; avoid feeding irrelevant history and oversized source passages.
3. **Prompt/prefix caching**, in memory only and scoped to exact model, policy, session and grant inputs; benchmark cold vs warm.
4. **Scheduling and queue discipline:** interactive priority, bounded concurrency and no background job blocking an interactive request.
5. **Constrained tool output** primarily improves schema reliability; measure whether it reduces retries and total task latency rather than assuming a raw decode speed gain.
6. **Retrieval/prefill overlap** only as an experiment. Memory-bandwidth contention can make total latency worse.

First instrument a full latency breakdown: queue wait, retrieval, prefill/TTFT, decode, tool round trips, validation, and time-to-first-useful-answer. Report p50/p95 by task class and context size, with cold/warm runs and concurrent indexing.

### 4. Speculative decoding

Keep deferred, not categorically rejected. MoE expert routing does not by itself prove poor draft acceptance; acceptance depends on target/draft distribution alignment and decoding settings. The official QAT collection's existence of assistant/draft checkpoints makes an experiment plausible, but no gain should be assumed. Only test after the primary MLX path is stable; compare acceptance rate, net tokens/sec, p95 latency, memory, and quality against ordinary decoding. If it adds memory pressure or complexity without a meaningful end-to-end win, drop it.

### 5. Deterministic router boundary

Acceptable: a fixed, versioned policy over **explicit structured signals** (typed UI capability, explicit Research mode, declared output schema, approved task template, context budget). It may choose a pre-approved profile and must log the reason and model identity.

Not acceptable under the ratified rule: using chat wording, embeddings, a classifier, or model self-confidence to infer that the user “probably only needs” a smaller model. Even a calibrated semantic router can silently route a request away from the expected reasoning path. Reopening that rule requires owner approval.

### 6. Owner-facing explanation

Use this wording; it avoids the “runs like a 4B” overclaim:

> “The selected primary is a 26B-total-parameter MoE that activates about 3.8B parameters per generated token. That can reduce decode work compared with a dense model of similar total size, but all model weights still need to remain in memory, and it does not guarantee 4B-model speed. A dense 14B is not automatically faster: it may use less memory but can read more weights per token. We have not measured either model on the target Mac. Atlas will first make routine operations fast by avoiding generation when deterministic code is sufficient, keeping context focused, and prioritising interactive work. We will only add another model if target-Mac measurements show a material user-visible benefit without an unacceptable quality or privacy trade-off.”

**Bottom line:** Claude's final decision 7071570360 is sound as the owner-delegated architecture/model selection. Treat it as the selected design, not as measured performance or release acceptance. The exact target-Mac acceptance gates remain binding. No code or docs changed in this response; no WP3.4 started.

Source: https://github.com/Prasanna666666/Prasanna/pull/8#issuecomment-7071603144

|---|---|---|
| Memory left after macOS + apps (~5–6 GB) and Atlas (~1.5–2 GB) [E] | **~24 GB** | **~16.5 GB** |
| Primary (Gemma 26B-A4B ≈ 16.6–18.6 GB with KV and runtime [E]) | fits, ~5–7 GB spare | **does not fit at useful context** |
| GPT-OSS-20B (≈ 14.3–16.7 GB [E]) | fits, ~7–10 GB spare | borderline, no room for voice, image or concurrency |
| Dense 31B deep tier (≈ 17.5 GB weights) | swap-in possible | **cannot run** |
| Decode speed for a model that fits | lower bandwidth | up to ~1.8× theoretical ceiling (not a guarantee: kernels, MoE routing, KV traffic, thermals) |
| Concurrency (cap 2) and 32K context, Atlas services, voice/vision | comfortable | tight |
| Upgrade path | none (memory is fixed at purchase) | none |
The Neural Engine is not used by MLX LLM inference [M]; M5/M6 GPU "Neural Accelerator" benefit for MLX is **[?] unverified**. Thermals for a mini under sustained load are unmeasured on both. The external SSD adds storage, never memory.
**Decision: M6 32 GB.** It is the only one of the two that holds the primary with real context, the deep-tier swap and Atlas's own services. M5 Pro's bandwidth advantage is real, but it is spent on a model that barely fits.

### Strongest arguments against my recommendation
1. **Bandwidth is the speed lever** and the owner's goal is fast: a 1.8× advantage is large. If Atlas ran only one compact MoE, M5 Pro 24 GB would be faster. My answer: the intended workload is not one model; 24 GB removes the primary at 32K+ context.
2. **A fatal gap in the comparison: Apple's spec pages list M5 Pro configurations with 32 GB [V/S, one page conflicting].** If an **M5 Pro 32 GB** is within budget, it **dominates M6 32 GB** (same memory, ~1.8× bandwidth, more cores). The owner's alternative was 24 GB, but price was not stated and I could not find prices **[?]**.
3. **MoE quality at ~3.6–3.8B active may be too thin** for research and debate; handled by the pre-decided deep tier and gates, not by hardware.
4. **GPT-OSS-20B as primary** has the smaller footprint and a mature reasoning/tool-use design; if Dutch and image needs are lighter than assumed it could win on speed and headroom.

### Minimum validation before purchase and deployment (nothing more)
1. **Price check (decisive):** confirm the current price of **M5 Pro 32 GB**. If it is within budget, **buy it instead of M6 32 GB**; otherwise buy M6 32 GB. Do not buy 24 GB.
2. **Buy within the return window; day-1 smoke test** (about an hour): load the primary at 32K context, a 20-minute sustained run, record memory pressure, swap (must be zero), time to first token and decode speed. This tests the four disqualifiers in the recorded decision; fail means the fallback chain, not a redesign.
3. **Before real data:** read the licence texts and model-card or technical-report lineage statements for the finalists (the stated disqualifiers).

### Routing and escalation (minimal, robust; as ratified)
One resident primary. Typed capabilities skip the model; free-text chat always enters it with retrieval first; no semantic router. Deep tier loads only by owner-selected Research mode or a deterministic trigger (validation failure, missing or conflicting evidence, declared high-stakes class), disclosed, with minimum evidence carried and grants revalidated before use. No second large model runs concurrently: a 12.8 GiB model + a ~8 GB 14B + a ~17.8 GB 30B cannot co-reside on 32 GB (the first two alone are ≈ 33.6 GB with macOS, Atlas, KV and runtime [E]) and on 24 GB not even two.

### Security and privacy: what to check, not "safe"
- **Provenance:** a manifest per model (developer jurisdiction, base lineage, upstream ID and revision, licence text, quant source and conversion method, SHA-256), full digest on import and remount, load bound to verified read-only bytes (decided).
- **Weights format:** load **safetensors or GGUF only**; no pickle, no `trust_remote_code`; pin runtime and package versions; install from verified hashes into a dedicated environment.
- **Offline enforcement:** run inference in a **separate process under a network-deny sandbox**, with the Hugging Face offline mode (`HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1`) and update/telemetry paths disabled; verify with a test that observes the process opens **no network socket** during load and inference. Any download happens only in an explicit, owner-approved import step.
- **Not checked yet:** no primary licence, card, config or telemetry behaviour was read; nothing is labelled safe.

### Reconciliation with the accepted baseline
The baseline says "~27B main-model class selected by benchmark". **Gemma 4 26B-A4B matches it; GPT-OSS-20B (21B) is below it; Ministral 3 14B and Nemotron are not "main-class".** The recorded decision already narrowed "automatic switching by task" to explicit modes. Your 20B + 14B + 30B stack is **worse** than the recorded one on speed (the "fast" model is the slowest), memory (a zoo that cannot co-reside), lineage evidence (Nemotron unverified) and complexity; it is **better** only in giving GPT-OSS-20B equal billing, which the fallback chain already does.

### Claims I cannot verify from available evidence
Any tokens/sec or latency on either Mac; MLX support and stability for Gemma 4, Ministral 3 14B and the hybrid Nemotron; M5 Pro and M6 32 GB prices; the M5 Pro 32 GB option's exact configuration; Gemma 4 and GPT-OSS training-data provenance; licence texts (only reported as Apache 2.0 or NVIDIA Open Model License); GPU Neural Accelerator benefit under MLX; sustained thermals.
